, Lemma 5.10]
.

6.
Proof of Theorem
1.1
and of Theorem
1.2

6.1.
Summary of the properties of the Markov extension

We present a summary of the objects we have constructed so far, and their properties.

We have constructed a family of sets
\mathcal{Z}
s.t

(1)

\mathcal{Z}
is
countable
(Definition
5.1
; see also Proposition
2.20
and Definition
2.23
).

(2)

\mathcal{Z}
covers (exactly)
\mathrm{RST}
(Corollary
4.10
).

(3)

\mathcal{Z}
is
locally finite
:
\forall Z\in\mathcal{Z},\#\{Z^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Z}:Z\cap Z^{\prime}\neq\varnothing\}<\infty
(Theorem
5.2
).

(4)

Every
Z\in\mathcal{Z}
has
product structure
: There are subsets
W^{s}(x,Z),W^{u}(x,Z)\subset Z

(x\in Z)

s.t

(a)

x\in W^{s}(x,Z)\cap W^{u}(x,Z)
.

(b)

\forall x,y\in Z\exists!z\in Z
s.t
W^{u}(x,Z)\cap W^{s}(y,Z)=\{z\}
, we write
z=[x,y]_{Z}.

(c)

\forall x,y\in Z

W^{u}(x,Z),W^{u}(y,Z)
are equal or disjoint (similarly for
W^{s}(x,Z),W^{s}(y,z)
).

(Proposition
5.4
, Proposition
5.5
).

(5)

The
symbolic Markov property
: if
x\in Z(u_{0}),f(x)\in Z(u_{1})
, and
u_{0}\rightarrow u_{1}
, then
f[W^{s}(x,Z_{0})]\subset W^{s}(f(x),Z_{1})
and
f^{-1}[W^{u}(f(x),Z_{1})]\subset W^{u}(x,Z_{0})
(Proposition
5.9
).
9
9

9

To apply Proposition
5.9
, represent
x=\pi(v)
with
v\in\Sigma^{\#}
,
v_{0}=u_{0}
, and
f(x)=\pi(w)
with
w\in\Sigma^{\#}
,
w_{0}=u_{1}
; and note that
x=\pi(u),u\in\Sigma^{\#}
where
u_{i}=v_{i}

(i\leq 0)
,
u_{i}=w_{i-1}

(i\geq 1)
.

(6)

Uniform expansion/contraction on
W^{s}(x,Z),W^{u}(x,Z)
:
\forall Z\in\mathcal{Z},\forall z\in Z
,
\forall x\in W^{s}(z,Z),y\in W^{u}(z,Z)
,
\forall n\geq 0
,
d(f^{n}(x),f^{n}(z)),d(f^{-n}(y),f^{-n}(z))\leq 4I^{-n}(1)
(Proposition
4.4
(1)).

(7)

Bowen’s property with finite degree
(see
[
BB17
, Definition 1.11]
):

(a)

\forall u,v\in\Sigma:

\Big(\forall i,Z(u_{i})\cap Z(v_{i})\neq\varnothing\Big)\Rightarrow\pi(u)=\pi(v)
(Proposition
3.14
(4), proof of Proposition
4.12
).

(b)

\forall u,v\in\Sigma^{\#}
:
\pi(u)=\pi(v)\Leftrightarrow\Big(Z(u_{i})\cap Z(v_{i})\neq\varnothing

\forall i\in\mathbb{Z}\Big)
((a), Definition
5.1
).

(c)

\forall Z\in\mathcal{Z}
:
\#\{Z^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Z}:Z^{\prime}\cap Z\neq\emptyset\}<\infty
(Theorem
5.2
).

For the sake of completeness, we present below the refinement of
\mathcal{Z}
into a partition, while the same arguments appear in verbatim in
[
BO18
, § 6]
and beforehand in
[
Sar13
]
. This refinment is used to prove Theorem
1.1
and Theorem
1.2
.

6.2.
Construction of a partition with a finite-to-one almost everywhere coding

We explain how the results which are listed in §
6.1
are sufficient to prove Theorem
1.1
and Theorem
1.2
.

Step 1:

\mathcal{Z}
can be refined into a countable
partition

\mathcal{R}
with the following properties:

(a)

Product structure.

(b)

Symbolic Markov property.

(c)

Every
Z\in\mathcal{Z}
contains only finitely many
R\in\mathcal{R}
.

Proof:
This is done exactly as in
[
Sar13
, § 11]
, using the Bowen-Sinai refinement procedure
[
Bow70
,
Bow08
,
Bow78
]
, the local-finiteness property (3), and the product structure (4) of
Z_{i}\in\mathcal{Z}
. The partition elements are the equivalence classes of the following equivalence relation on
\bigcup\mathcal{Z}
(see
[
Sar13
, Proposition 11.2]
):

x\sim y\iff\forall Z,Z^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Z},\begin{bmatrix}x\in Z\iff y\in Z\\
W^{u}(x,Z)\cap Z^{\prime}\neq\varnothing\iff W^{u}(y,Z)\cap Z^{\prime}\neq\varnothing\\
W^{s}(x,Z)\cap Z^{\prime}\neq\varnothing\iff W^{s}(y,Z)\cap Z^{\prime}\neq\varnothing\end{bmatrix}.

Step 2:
For every finite chain
R_{0}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow R_{m}
,
\bigcap_{j=0}^{m}f^{-j}[R_{j}]\neq\varnothing
.

Proof:
This follows from the fact that
\mathcal{R}
is a Markov partition, using a classical argument due to Adler and Weiss, and Sinai. The proof is by induction on
m
. Assume the statement holds for
m
, and prove for
m+1
: Take
p\in f^{m}\Big[\bigcap_{j=0}^{m}f^{-j}[R_{j}]\Big]
, and take
p^{\prime}\in R_{m}\cap f^{-1}R_{m+1}
.
p,p^{\prime}\in R_{m}
, whence belong to some
Z\in\mathcal{Z}
; therefore
[p,p^{\prime}]_{Z}
is well defined by Definition
5.6
and Proposition
5.5
. By the symbolic Markov property,
f^{-m}([p,p^{\prime}]_{Z})\in\bigcap_{j=0}^{m+1}f^{-j}[R_{j}]
.

Step 3:
Let
\widehat{\mathcal{G}}
be a directed graph with set of vertices
\mathcal{R}
and set of edges
\{(R,S)\in\mathcal{R}\times\mathcal{R}:R\cap f^{-1}[S]\neq\varnothing\}
. Let
\widehat{\Sigma}:=\Sigma(\widehat{\mathcal{G}})
and define
\widehat{\pi}:\widehat{\Sigma}\rightarrow M
by
\{\widehat{\pi}(\underline{R})\}=\bigcap\limits_{n\geq 0}\overline{\bigcap\limits_{i=-n}^{n}f^{-i}[R_{i}]}
. Then
\widehat{\pi}
is well defined, and:

(a)

\widehat{\pi}[\widehat{\Sigma}]\supset\mathrm{RST}
.

(b)

\widehat{\pi}\circ\sigma=f\circ\widehat{\pi}
.

(c)

\widehat{\pi}
is uniformly continuous.

(d)

\widehat{\Sigma}
is locally compact.

Proof:
This proof is a concise presentation of
[
Sar13
, Theorem 12.3, theorem 12.5]
. Step 2 guarantees that the intersection in the expression defining
\{\widehat{\pi}\}
is not empty. The intersection is a singleton, because
\mathrm{diam}(\bigcap_{i=-n}^{n}f^{-i}[R_{i}])\leq 8I^{-n}(1)
. To see this, fix
x,y\in\bigcap_{i=-n}^{n}f^{-i}[R_{i}]
, and fix
\underline{u}\in\Sigma^{\#}
s.t
\pi(\underline{u})=x
. It follows that
\bigcap_{i=-n}^{n}f^{-i}[R_{i}]\subset\bigcap_{i=-n}^{n}f^{-i}[Z(u_{i})]
, whence
y\in\bigcap_{i=-n}^{n}f^{-i}[Z(u_{i})]
. Let
z:=[x,y]_{Z(u_{0})}
. By (4) and (5),
z\in\bigcap_{i=-n}^{n}f^{-i}[Z(u_{i})]
,
f^{n}(z)\in W^{u}(f^{n}(x),Z(u_{n}))
,
f^{-n}(z)\in W^{s}(f^{-n}(y),Z(u_{-n}))
, whence by (6)
d(x,y)\leq d(x,z)+d(z,y)=d(f^{-n}(f^{n}(x)),f^{-n}(f^{n}(z)))+d(f^{n}(f^{-n}(z)),f^{n}(f^{-n}(y)))\leq 8I^{-n}(1)
.
Therefore
\widehat{\pi}
is well defined.

(a)

Let
x\in\mathrm{RST}
. By Theorem
3.15
,
f^{i}(x)\in\pi[\Sigma^{\#}]
for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
. By definition
\mathcal{Z}
is a cover of
\pi[\Sigma^{\#}]
, and
\mathcal{R}
refines
\mathcal{Z}
; whence
f^{i}(x)\in\mathop{\vphantom{\bigcup}\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\displaystyle#$\hfil\cr\bigcup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\textstyle#$\hfil\cr\bigcup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\bigcup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptscriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\bigcup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}}\displaylimits\mathcal{R}

\forall i\in\mathbb{Z}
. Denote by
R_{i}
the unique partition member of
\mathcal{R}
which contains
f^{i}(x)
.
\underline{R}
is an admissible chain by definition, and
\widehat{\pi}(\underline{R})=x
. Let
\underline{u}\in\Sigma^{\#}
s.t
\pi(\underline{u})=x
, then
Z(u_{i})\supset R_{i}

\forall i\in\mathbb{Z}
.
\mathcal{Z}
is locally-finite, so by the pigeonhole principle,
\vec{R}
must be in
\widehat{\Sigma}^{\#}
.

(b)

\{(f\circ\widehat{\pi})(\underline{R})\}=\bigcap_{n\geq 0}\bigcap_{i=-n}^{n}\overline{f^{-(i-1)}[R_{i-1+1}]}=\{\widehat{\pi}(\sigma\underline{R})\}\text{ }(\because f\text{ is a diffeomorphism}).

(c)

Suppose
d(\underline{R},\underline{S})=e^{-n}
, then
R_{i}=S_{i}
for
|i|\leq n
, whence
\widehat{\pi}(\underline{R}),\widehat{\pi}(\underline{S})\in\overline{\bigcap_{i=-n}^{n}f^{-i}[R_{i}]}
. We just saw in the beginning of the proof of step 3 that
\mathrm{diam}(\bigcap_{i=-n}^{n}f^{-i}[R_{i}])\leq 8I^{-n}(1)
. So
d(\widehat{\pi}(\underline{R}),\widehat{\pi}(\underline{S}))\leq 8\cdot I^{\lceil\log d(\underline{R},\underline{S})\rceil}(1)
.

(d)

Fix a vertex
R\in\mathcal{R}
. Assume
R\rightarrow S
. Let
u\in\mathcal{V}
s.t
R\subset Z(u)
. Take
x\in R\cap f^{-1}[S]\subset Z(u)
, then
\exists\underline{u}\in\Sigma^{\#}
s.t
u_{0}=u
and
\pi(\underline{u})=x
. Then
f(x)\in S\cap Z(u_{1})
. Thus, by step 1(c), and Lemma
2.24
,

\#\{S\in\mathcal{R}:R\rightarrow S\}\leq\sum_{u\in\mathcal{V}:R\subset Z(u)}\sum_{v\in\mathcal{V}:u\rightarrow v}\#\{S\in\mathcal{R}:S\subset Z(v)\}<\infty.

A similar calculation shows
\#\{S^{\prime}:S^{\prime}\rightarrow R\}<\infty
.

Step 4:

\widehat{\pi}[\widehat{\Sigma}^{\#}]=\mathrm{RST}
, and
\widehat{\pi}|_{\widehat{\Sigma}^{\#}}
is finite-to-one.

Proof:
In Corollary
4.10
we saw
\bigcup\mathcal{Z}=\pi[\Sigma^{\#}]=\mathrm{RST}
.
\mathcal{R}
is a refinement of
\mathcal{Z}
.
[
BO21
, Proposition 4.11]
shows
\widehat{\pi}[\widehat{\Sigma}^{\#}]=\pi[\Sigma^{\#}]
(with our
\epsilon>0
)
10
10

10

The proof of
[
BO21
, Proposition 4.11]
does not depend on
\epsilon
at all;
\epsilon
only appears in the statement to satisfy the equality
\pi[\Sigma^{\#}]=\mathrm{RST}
which we have already seen independently in Corollary
4.10
.
, and completes the proof that
\widehat{\pi}[\widehat{\Sigma}^{\#}]=\mathrm{RST}
. The finite-to-one property follows from Theorem
1.2
, whose proof follows verbatim the proof of
[
BO18
, Theorem 1.3]
(which follows the proof of Sarig in
[
Sar13
]
, which in turn adapts the earlier works of Bowen
[
Bow70
,
Bow71
,
Bow78
]
).

Proposition 6.1
.

For every
x\in\widehat{\pi}[\widehat{\Sigma}]
,
T_{x}M=E^{s}(x)\oplus E^{u}(x)
, where

(1)\text{ }\sum_{n\geq 0}\|d_{x}f^{n}|_{E^{s}(x)}\|^{2}<\infty\text{ , }(2)\text{ }\sum_{n\geq 0}\|d_{x}f^{-n}|_{E^{u}(x)}\|^{2}<\infty.

The maps
\underline{R}\mapsto E^{s/u}(\widehat{\pi}(\underline{R}))
have summable variations as maps from
\widehat{\Sigma}
to
TM
.

Proof.

The proof is similar to
[
Sar13
, Proposition 12.6]
, so as in
[
BO18
, Proposition 6.1]
, we limit ourselves to a brief sketch; One first writes
x
as the image of a chain
\underline{u}
in
\Sigma
(it is possible since the partition
\mathcal{R}
is a refinement of the cover
\mathcal{Z}
). Then the tangent space
T_{x}M
splits by
T_{x}\widecheck{V}^{s}(\underline{u})\oplus T_{x}\widecheck{V}^{u}(\underline{u})
. The rest is clear by Lemma
4.4
(2) and Proposition
3.14
(5).
∎

7.
Absolute Continuity of Weak Foliations

In §
3
we constructed local stable and unstable leaves for points in
\mathrm{RST}
. In fact, by using a strengthened method of the Graph Transform, we constructed local stable and unstable manifolds for every chain, while Theorem
3.15

says that every point in
\mathrm{RST}
can be coded by a chain in
\Sigma^{\#}
.

These local stable manifolds contract forwards in time, as shown in Proposition
4.4
. However, they may contract in a strictly slower than an exponential rate, which distinguishes their behavior from the behavior of classical local stable leaves of non-uniformly hyperbolic orbits. For that reason, the foliations by such leaves are called
weak foliations
. In an upcoming paper, we present a few examples of systems which exhibit orbits with
strictly weak foliations
, while these orbits are shown to carry invariant measures of interest (alas not probability measures, as every invariant probability measure carried by
\mathrm{RST}
is hyperbolic), and we show furthermore that these orbits have full Riamannian volume.

In this section of the paper we wish to prove that weak foliations are absolutely continuous w.r.t the holonomy map and the Riemannian volume.
11
11

11

This terminology is common- however may be slightly misleading; since in the general case the stable leaves may not generate a foliation, and may not exist for every point.
This property was shown by Anosov and Sinai for the stable foliations of Anosov systems in
[
AS67
]
, and later extended by Pesin to the non-uniformly hyperbolic setup (see
[
BP07
, § 8.6]
for proof). Pesin’s proof deals with the difficulty that not every point may admit a local stable leaf, and with the fact that the size of local stable leaves may vary greatly in small neighborhoods. Pesin’s approach is to restrict to a regular set (i.e Pesin block), and consider the leaves which pass through points in a fixed such set.

The proof we present below follows the idea of Pesin, while utilizing one more property: A careful examination of Pesin’s proof shows that a minimal requirement for his scheme to work is that the leaves contract fast enough so that the sequence of their diameters over a forwards-orbit is summable. In our proof, restricting to a partition element takes the role of restricting to a regular set. Proposition
4.4
and Lemma
2.6
show the summability property on partition elements, which is needed for the scheme to work. Although, one still has to show that the
\beta
-Hölder continuity of
d_{\cdot}f
is sufficient for summability when
\beta
may be arbitrarily small, which was obvious for the exponentially contracting rate in Pesin’s case.

For the simplicity of presentation we prove the absolute continuity of the holonomy map between two unstable leaves of chains in a fixed chart; but the proof easily extends for two admissible
u
-manifolds in the chart similarly (recall Definition
3.2
). The principle which is used is that the contraction on the stable leaves overpowers any possible contraction in the transversals in a summable manner.

7.1.
Bounded leaf measures

Let
\mathcal{V}
be the collection of double charts from Definition
2.23
. Fix
u\in\mathcal{V}
, and the set
A(u):=\pi[\Sigma\cap[u]]
. Notice,
A(u)
is compact and
A(u)\supseteq Z(u)
which is covered by finitely many partition elements (recall §
6.2
). Fix
(u_{i}^{1})_{i\leq 0}
and
(u_{i}^{2})_{i\leq 0}
two negative chains s.t
u^{1}_{0}=u^{2}_{0}=u=\psi_{x}^{p^{s},p^{u}}
. Denote the respective representative functions of
V^{u}((u_{i}^{1})_{i\leq 0})
and
V^{u}((u_{i}^{2})_{i\leq 0})
by
F^{1}
and
F^{2}
.

Notice: every point in
V^{u}((u_{i}^{j})_{i\leq 0})\cap A(u)
can be coded by a chain
\underline{u}\in\Sigma
s.t
u_{i}^{j}=u_{i}
for all
i\leq 0
and
j=1,2
.

Lemma 7.1
.

Let
x\in V^{u}((u_{i}^{1})_{i\leq 0})\cap Z(u)
, and let
\underline{u}\in\Sigma
s.t
u_{i}^{j}=u_{i}
for all
i\leq 0
and
\pi(\underline{u})=x
. Write
y:=\pi((u_{i}^{2})_{i\leq 0}\cdot(u_{i})_{i\geq 0})
, where
\cdot
denotes an admissible concatenation. Write
u_{i}=\psi_{x_{i}}^{p^{s}_{i},p^{u}_{i}}
,
i\in\mathbb{Z}
.

For every
n\geq 0
, let
\underline{u}^{1,n}:=(u_{i}^{1})_{i\leq 0}\cdot(u_{j})_{j=0}^{n}
and
\underline{u}^{2,n}:=(u_{i}^{2})_{i\leq 0}\cdot(u_{j})_{j=0}^{n}

F_{n}^{1}
, and let
F_{n}^{2}
be the representative functions of
V^{u}(\underline{u}^{1,n})
and
V^{u}(\underline{u}^{2,n})
, respectively. Write
\widetilde{F}^{j}_{n}(v):=(F^{j}_{n}(v),v)
,
n\geq 0
.

Then for all
n\geq 0
, for all
\eta\in(0,(I^{-n}(p^{s}\wedge p^{u}))^{2})
,

\left|\frac{\mathrm{Vol}(\psi_{x_{n}}\circ\widetilde{F}^{1}_{n}[R_{\eta}(0)])}{\mathrm{Vol}(\psi_{x_{n}}\circ\widetilde{F}^{2}_{n}[R_{\eta}(0)])}-1\right|\leq\epsilon^{2}(I^{-n}(p^{s}\wedge p^{u}))^{\frac{1}{\gamma}+\tau^{\prime}},

where
\tau^{\prime}=\frac{1}{4\gamma}>0
,
\mathrm{Vol}
denotes the intrinsic volume of a submanifold with an induced Riemannian volume, and
R_{\eta}(0)
is an
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
-norm ball of radius
\eta
around
0
in
\mathbb{R}^{u(x)}
.

Proof.

We fix
n\geq 0
, and omit the subscript in the proof where it is clear, as all analysis is in a fixed chart
u_{n}=\psi_{x_{n}}^{p^{s}_{n},p^{u}_{n}}
.

Step 1:
Define

I:\psi_{x_{n}}(F^{1}(t),t)\mapsto\psi_{x_{n}}(F^{2}(t),t).

Equivalently,
I=\psi_{x_{n}}\circ\tilde{F}^{2}\circ\pi_{u}\circ\psi_{x_{n}}^{-1}
, and
\tilde{F}^{1/2}
are diffeomorphisms onto their images, where
\pi_{u}
is the projection onto the
u
-coordinates. So
I
is a diffeomorphism from
\widecheck{V}^{u}(\underline{u}^{1,n})
to
\widecheck{V}^{u}(\underline{u}^{2,n})
.
I
satisfies

(55)

d(I(x^{\prime}),x^{\prime})\leq\|\psi_{x_{n}}\|d_{C^{0}}(F^{1},F^{2})\leq 2d_{C^{0}}(F^{1},F^{2}).

The identity map of
\widecheck{V}^{u}(\underline{u}^{1,n})
can be written as
\mathrm{Id}=\psi_{x_{n}}\circ\tilde{F}^{1}\circ\pi_{u}\circ\psi_{x_{n}}^{-1}:\widecheck{V}^{u}(\underline{u}^{1,n})\rightarrow\widecheck{V}^{u}(\underline{u}^{1,n})
. Let
z\in\widecheck{V}^{u}(\underline{u}^{1,n})
, and let
\Theta_{D}:TD\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a local isometry as in Definition
2.16
s.t.
D
is a neighborhood which contains
z,I(z)
. Then,

\displaystyle\|\Theta_{D}d_{z}I-\Theta_{D}d_{z}\mathrm{Id}\|

\displaystyle=\|\Theta_{D}d_{z}(\psi_{x_{n}}\circ\tilde{F}^{2}\circ\pi_{u}\circ\psi_{x_{n}}^{-1})-\Theta_{D}d_{z}(\psi_{x_{n}}\circ\tilde{F}^{1}\circ\pi_{u}\circ\psi_{x_{n}}^{-1})\|

\displaystyle=\|[\Theta_{D}d_{t}(\psi_{x_{n}}\circ\tilde{F}^{\underline{v}})-\Theta_{D}d_{t}(\psi_{x_{n}}\tilde{F}^{\underline{u}})]\pi_{u}\circ d_{z}(\psi_{x_{n}}^{-1}))\|,\hskip 7.11317pt\text{where }t=\pi_{u}\psi_{x_{n}}^{-1}(z),

\displaystyle\leq 2\|C_{0}^{-1}(x_{n})\|\cdot\|\Theta_{D}d_{t}(\psi_{x_{n}}\circ\tilde{F}^{2})-\Theta_{D}d_{t}(\psi_{x_{n}}\circ\tilde{F}^{1})\|

\displaystyle=2\|C_{0}^{-1}(x_{n})\|\cdot\|\Theta_{D}d_{\tilde{F}^{2}(t)}\psi_{x_{n}}d_{t}\tilde{F}^{2}-\Theta_{D}d_{\tilde{F}^{1}(t)}\psi_{x_{n}}d_{t}\tilde{F}^{1}\|

\displaystyle=2\|C_{0}^{-1}(x_{n})\|\cdot\|\Theta_{D}d_{\tilde{F}^{\underline{v}}(t)}\psi_{x_{n}}(d_{t}\tilde{F}^{2}-d_{t}\tilde{F}^{1})+(\Theta_{D}d_{\tilde{F}^{2}(t)}\psi_{x_{n}}-\Theta_{D}d_{\tilde{F}^{1}(t)}\psi_{x_{n}})d_{t}\tilde{F}^{1}\|

\displaystyle\leq 2\|C_{0}^{-1}(x_{n})\|\cdot\left[d_{C^{1}}(\tilde{F}^{1},\tilde{F}^{2})\|d_{\cdot}\psi_{x_{n}}\|+\mathrm{Lip}(d_{\cdot}\psi_{x_{n}})d_{C^{0}}(\tilde{F}^{1},\tilde{F}^{2})\cdot\|d_{\cdot}\tilde{F}^{1}\|\right]

\displaystyle\leq 8\|C_{0}^{-1}(x_{n})\|\cdot d_{C^{1}}(\widecheck{V}^{u}(\underline{u}^{1,n}),\widecheck{V}^{u}(\underline{u}^{2,n}))\leq 8(C_{\beta,\epsilon}^{-1}\cdot p_{n}^{s}\wedge p_{n}^{u})^{\frac{-1}{2\gamma}}\cdot d_{C^{1}}(\widecheck{V}^{u}(\underline{u}^{1,n}),\widecheck{V}^{u}(\underline{u}^{2,n}))

(56)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 8(C_{\beta,\epsilon}^{-1}\cdot I^{-n}(p^{s}\wedge p^{u}))^{\frac{-1}{2\gamma}}\cdot d_{C^{1}}(\widecheck{V}^{u}(\underline{u}^{1,n}),\widecheck{V}^{u}(\underline{u}^{2,n})).

It follows that,

\displaystyle|\mathrm{Jac}(d_{z}I)-1|=

\displaystyle|\mathrm{Jac}(d_{z}I)-\mathrm{Jac}(d_{z}\mathrm{Id})|\leq C_{1}\|\Theta_{D}d_{z}I-\Theta_{D}d_{z}\mathrm{Id}\|

(57)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 8C_{1}(C_{\beta,\epsilon}^{-1}\cdot I^{-n}(p^{s}\wedge p^{u}))^{\frac{-1}{2\gamma}}\cdot d_{C^{1}}(\widecheck{V}^{u}(\underline{u}^{1,n}),\widecheck{V}^{u}(\underline{u}^{2,n})),

where
C_{1}
is the Lipschitz constant for the absolute value of the determinant on the ball of
(d\times d)
-matrices with an operator norm bounded by
2
, where
\|d_{z}I\|\leq 2
by (
7.1
).
12
12

12

Write
u=dimV^{u}(\underline{R})
, and let
A,B
be
u\times u
matrices s.t.
|a_{ij}-b_{ij}|\leq\delta
for all
i,j\leq u
. So
\det A=\sum_{\sigma\in S_{u}}\mathrm{sgn}(\sigma)\prod_{i=1}^{u}a_{i\sigma(i)}
. Then
|\det A-\det B|\leq\sum_{\sigma\in S_{u}}|\prod_{i=1}^{u}a_{i\sigma(i)}-\prod_{i=1}^{u}b_{i\sigma(i)}|\leq|S_{u}|\cdot u\|B\|_{Fr}^{u-1}\delta
. Take maximum over
u\leq d-1
.

By Proposition
3.14
,
d_{C^{1}}(\widecheck{V}^{u}(\underline{u}^{1,n}),\widecheck{V}^{u}(\underline{u}^{2,n}))\leq C(I^{-n}(p^{s}\wedge p^{u}))^{\frac{1}{\gamma}+\tau}
, for
C,\tau>0
constants depending on the calibration parameters (but not on
\epsilon
). Furthermore, by (
18
) in Proposition
3.14
, when
\gamma>\frac{5}{\beta}
,
\beta-\frac{2}{\gamma}>\frac{3}{\gamma}=(\frac{1}{\gamma}+\tau^{\prime})+(\frac{1}{\gamma}+\frac{1}{2\gamma}+\tau^{\prime})
, where
\tau^{\prime}:=\frac{1}{4\gamma}>0
. Then for
C^{\prime}:=3\sup_{N\geq 0}\{N\cdot(I^{-N}(1))^{\frac{1}{\gamma}+\tau^{\prime}}\}<\infty
, we get

d_{C^{1}}(\widecheck{V}^{u}(\underline{u}^{1,n}),\widecheck{V}^{u}(\underline{u}^{2,n}))\leq C^{\prime}(I^{-n}(p^{s}\wedge p^{u}))^{\frac{1}{\gamma}+\frac{1}{2\gamma}+\tau^{\prime}}.

Substituting this in (
7.1
) gives, for all sufficiently small
\epsilon>0
,

(58)

|\mathrm{Jac}(d_{z}I)-1|\leq 8C_{1}C_{\beta,\epsilon}^{\frac{1}{2\gamma}}\cdot C^{\prime}(I^{-n}(p^{s}\wedge p^{u}))^{\frac{1}{\gamma}+\tau^{\prime}}\leq\epsilon^{2}(I^{-n}(p^{s}\wedge p^{u}))^{\frac{1}{\gamma}+\tau^{\prime}}.

Step 2:

\displaystyle|\mathrm{Vol}(\psi_{x_{n}}\circ\widetilde{F}^{1}[R_{\eta}(0)])-\mathrm{Vol}(\psi_{x_{n}}\circ\widetilde{F}^{2}[R_{\eta}(0)])|=

\displaystyle|\int\limits_{R_{\eta}(0)}\mathrm{Jac}(d_{t}(\psi_{x_{n}}\tilde{F}^{1}))d\mathrm{Leb}(t)-\int_{R_{\eta}(0)}\mathrm{Jac}(d_{t}(\psi_{x_{n}}\tilde{F}^{2}))d\mathrm{Leb}(t)|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{R_{\eta}(0)}|\mathrm{Jac}(d_{t}(\psi_{x_{n}}\tilde{F}^{1}))-\mathrm{Jac}(d_{t}(\psi_{x_{n}}\tilde{F}^{2}))|d\mathrm{Leb}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\limits_{R_{\eta}(0)}\mathrm{Jac}(d_{t}(\psi_{x_{n}}\widetilde{F}^{1}))\cdot|1-\mathrm{Jac}(d_{t}(\psi_{x_{n}}\tilde{F}^{2}))

\displaystyle\cdot\mathrm{Jac}(d_{\psi_{x_{n}}\tilde{F}^{1}(t)}((\psi_{x_{n}}\tilde{F}^{1})^{-1}))|d\mathrm{Leb}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\limits_{R_{\eta}(0)}\mathrm{Jac}(d_{t}(\psi_{x_{n}}\widetilde{F}^{1}))\cdot|1-\mathrm{Jac}(d_{\psi_{x_{n}}\tilde{F}^{1}(t)}I)|d\mathrm{Leb}(t).

By (
58
),

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\mathrm{Vol}(\psi_{x_{n}}\circ\widetilde{F}^{2}[R_{\eta}(0)])}{\mathrm{Vol}(\psi_{x_{n}}\circ\widetilde{F}^{1}[R_{\eta}(0)])}-1\right|=

\displaystyle\frac{|\mathrm{Vol}(\psi_{x_{n}}\circ\widetilde{F}^{1}[R_{\eta}(0)])-\mathrm{Vol}(\psi_{x_{n}}\circ\widetilde{F}^{2}[R_{\eta}(0)])|}{\mathrm{Vol}(\psi_{x_{n}}\circ\widetilde{F}^{1}[R_{\eta}(0)])}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{\epsilon^{2}(I^{-n}(p^{s}\wedge p^{u}))^{\frac{1}{\gamma}+\tau^{\prime}}\int_{R_{\eta}(0)}\mathrm{Jac}(d_{t}(\psi_{x_{n}}\circ\widetilde{F}^{1}[R_{\eta}(0)])\mathrm{Leb}(t)}{\int_{R_{\eta}(0)}\mathrm{Jac}(d_{t}(\psi_{x_{n}}\circ\widetilde{F}^{1}[R_{\eta}(0)])\mathrm{Leb}(t)}

(59)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\epsilon^{2}(I^{-n}(p^{s}\wedge p^{u}))^{\frac{1}{\gamma}+\tau^{\prime}}.

∎

Lemma 7.2
.

In the notation of Lemma
7.1
,
\forall n\geq 0

\frac{|d_{f^{n}(x)}f^{-n}\omega_{u}(f^{n}(x))|}{|d_{f^{n}(y)}f^{-n}\omega_{u}(f^{n}(y))|}=e^{\pm\epsilon^{2}(\frac{d(x,y)}{(p^{s}\wedge p^{u})^{3}})^{\frac{\beta}{4}}},

where
\omega_{u}(\cdot)
is the normalized volume form of
T_{\cdot}V^{u}(\cdot)
.

Proof.

Showing
\frac{|d_{x}f^{n}\omega_{u}(x)|}{|d_{y}f^{n}\omega_{u}(y)|}=e^{\pm\epsilon^{2}(\frac{d(x,y)}{(p^{s}\wedge p^{u})^{3}})^{\frac{\beta}{4}}}
is enough. Proposition
4.4
treats the case for the normalized volume forms of
T_{\cdot}V^{s}(\cdot)
. The case for the normalized volume forms of
T_{\cdot}V^{u}(\cdot)
is similar.
∎

Corollary 7.3
.

In the notation of Lemma
7.1
,
\forall n\geq 0
sufficiently large (w.r.t
d(x,y)
and
p^{s}\wedge p^{u}
),
\forall\eta\in(0,(I^{-n}(p^{s}\wedge p^{u}))^{3+\frac{4}{\beta}(\frac{1}{\gamma}+\tau^{\prime})}]
,

\frac{\mathrm{Vol}(f^{-n}[\psi_{x_{n}}\circ\widetilde{F}^{1}_{n}[R_{\eta}(0)]])}{\mathrm{Vol}(f^{-n}[\psi_{x_{n}}\circ\widetilde{F}^{2}_{n}[R_{\eta}(0)]])}=e^{\pm 2\epsilon^{2}(\frac{d(x,y)}{(p^{s}\wedge p^{u})^{3}})^{\frac{\beta}{4}}},

where
\tau^{\prime}=\frac{1}{4\gamma}>0
.

Proof.

For any
t_{1},t_{2}\in R_{\eta}(0)
,
d(\psi_{x_{n}}(t_{1}),\psi_{x_{n}}(t_{2}))\leq\frac{3}{2}\eta
. We have,

\mathrm{Vol}(f^{-n}[\psi_{x_{n}}\circ\widetilde{F}^{1}_{n}[R_{\eta}(0)]])=\int_{\psi_{x_{n}}\circ\widetilde{F}^{1}_{n}[R_{\eta}(0)])}|d_{f^{n}(t)}f^{-n}\omega_{u}(f^{n}(t))|d\mathrm{Vol}_{V^{u}(f^{n}(x))}(t).

By Proposition
4.4
, part (3), since
\eta\leq I^{-n}(p^{s}\wedge p^{u}))^{3+\frac{4}{\beta}(\frac{1}{\gamma}+\tau^{\prime})}
and
(p^{s}_{n})^{3}\geq(p^{s}_{n}\wedge p^{u}_{n})^{3}\geq(I^{-n}(p^{s}\wedge p^{u}))^{3}
,

\mathrm{Vol}(f^{-n}[\psi_{x_{n}}\circ\widetilde{F}^{1}_{n}[R_{\eta}(0)]])=\mathrm{Vol}(\psi_{x_{n}}\circ\widetilde{F}^{1}_{n}[R_{\eta}(0)])\cdot|d_{f^{n}(x)}f^{-n}\omega_{u}(f^{n}(x))|e^{\pm\epsilon^{2}(\frac{3}{2})^{\frac{\beta}{4}}(I^{-n}(p^{s}\wedge p^{u}))^{\frac{1}{\gamma}+\tau^{\prime}}}.

Similarly,

\mathrm{Vol}(f^{-n}[\psi_{x_{n}}\circ\widetilde{F}^{2}_{n}[R_{\eta}(0)]])=\mathrm{Vol}(\psi_{x_{n}}\circ\widetilde{F}^{2}_{n}[R_{\eta}(0)])\cdot|d_{f^{n}(y)}f^{-n}\omega_{u}(f^{n}(y))|e^{\pm\epsilon^{2}(\frac{3}{2})^{\frac{\beta}{4}}(I^{-n}(p^{s}\wedge p^{u}))^{\frac{1}{\gamma}+\tau^{\prime}}}.

By Lemma
7.2
,

\frac{\mathrm{Vol}(f^{-n}[\psi_{x_{n}}\circ\widetilde{F}^{2}_{n}[R_{\eta}(0)]])}{\mathrm{Vol}(f^{-n}[\psi_{x_{n}}\circ\widetilde{F}^{1}_{n}[R_{\eta}(0)]])}=\frac{\mathrm{Vol}(\psi_{x_{n}}\circ\widetilde{F}^{2}_{n}[R_{\eta}(0)])}{\mathrm{Vol}(\psi_{x_{n}}\circ\widetilde{F}^{1}_{n}[R_{\eta}(0)])}e^{\pm 2\cdot(\frac{3}{2})^{\frac{\beta}{4}}\epsilon^{2}(I^{-n}(p^{s}\wedge p^{u}))^{\frac{1}{\gamma}+\tau^{\prime}}}e^{\pm\epsilon^{2}(\frac{d(x,y)}{(p^{s}\wedge p^{u})^{3}})^{\frac{\beta}{4}}}.

By Lemma
7.1
, for all
\epsilon
sufficiently small,
\frac{\mathrm{Vol}(\psi_{x_{n}}\circ\widetilde{F}^{2}[R_{\eta}(0)])}{\mathrm{Vol}(\psi_{x_{n}}\circ\widetilde{F}^{1}[R_{\eta}(0)])}=e^{\pm(1+(3-2(\frac{3}{2})^{\frac{\beta}{4}}))\epsilon^{2}(I^{-n}(p^{s}\wedge p^{u}))^{\frac{1}{\gamma}+\tau^{\prime}}}
. Thus in total,
\frac{\mathrm{Vol}(f^{-n}[\psi_{x_{n}}\circ\widetilde{F}^{2}_{n}[R_{\eta}(0)]])}{\mathrm{Vol}(f^{-n}[\psi_{x_{n}}\circ\widetilde{F}^{1}_{n}[R_{\eta}(0)]])}=e^{\pm 2\epsilon^{2}(\frac{d(x,y)}{(p^{s}\wedge p^{u})^{3}})^{\frac{\beta}{4}}}
for all
n
sufficiently large.
∎

7.2.
Covering lemmas and the proof of the absolute continuity of weak foliations

Lemma 7.4

(Besicovitch-Pesin)
.

For all sufficiently large
n\geq 0
, there exist
\{x^{(i)}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}\subseteq A(u)
, and sets
\{D_{i}^{1}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
s.t

(1)

x^{(i)}=\pi(\underline{u}^{(i)})
where
u^{(i)}_{j}=u^{1}_{j}
,
j\geq 0
; and
u_{j}^{(i)}=\psi_{x_{j}^{(i)}}^{p^{s,i}_{j},p^{u,i}_{j}}
.

(2)

D_{i}^{1}\subseteq\psi_{x_{n}^{(i)}}\circ\widetilde{F}^{1,i}_{n}[R_{\eta}(0)]
where
F^{1,i}
is the representative function of
V^{u}((u^{(i)}_{j})_{j\leq n})
in
u_{n}^{(i)}
, and
\eta\in(0,(I^{-n}(p^{s}\wedge p^{u}))^{3+\frac{4}{\beta}(\frac{1}{\gamma}+\tau^{\prime})}]
,
\tau^{\prime}:=\frac{1}{4\gamma}>0
.

(3)

\forall i\neq i^{\prime}
,
\mathrm{Vol}(D_{i}^{1}\cap D_{i^{\prime}}^{1})=0
(
\mathrm{Vol}
is the intrinsic
f^{n}[V^{u}((u^{1}_{i})_{i\leq 0})]
-volume).

(4)

A(u)\cap V^{u}((u^{1}_{i})_{i\leq 0})\subseteq\bigcup_{i=1}^{\ell}f^{-n}[D_{i}^{1}]
.

(5)

There exist
R_{n}
, and a constant
c_{m}>0
depending on the dimension of
M
s.t for
\eta_{n}:=(I^{-n}(p^{s}\wedge p^{u}))^{3+\frac{4}{\beta}(\frac{1}{\gamma}+\tau^{\prime})}
, for all
1\leq i\leq\ell
,

B(x^{(i)},c_{m}R_{n})\subseteq D_{i}^{1}\subseteq f^{-n}[\psi_{x_{n}^{(i)}}\circ\widetilde{F}^{1,i}_{n}[R_{\eta_{n}}(0)]])\subseteq B(x^{(i)},R_{n}).

The collection depends on the leaf
V^{u}((u^{1}_{i})_{i\leq 0})
, but a collection
\{D_{i}^{2}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
with similar properties exists for
V^{u}((u^{2}_{i})_{i\leq 0})
, and constitutes a cover of
A(u)\cap V^{u}((u^{2}_{i})_{i\leq 0})
. In particular, the two covers share the same multiplicity
\ell<\infty
. In addition, the collections
\{D_{i}^{1/2}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
can be chosen in such a way that
\forall 1\leq i\leq\ell
,

(60)

\frac{\mathrm{Vol}(D_{i}^{1})}{\mathrm{Vol}(D_{i}^{2})}=e^{\pm\epsilon^{2}(I^{-n}(p^{s}\wedge p^{u}))^{\frac{1}{\gamma}+\tau^{\prime}}}.

For proof see
[
BP07
, Lemma 8.6.3]
. For the estimate of
(
60
)
use the Jacobian bounds of the map
I
from Lemma
7.1
.

Remark:
The collections
\{D_{i}^{1/2}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
depends on
n
. To ease notation we omit notating it, when dependence is clear by context. In addition, similarly.

Corollary 7.5
.

For all
n\geq 0
sufficiently large, for all
1\leq i\leq\ell
,

\frac{\mathrm{Vol}(f^{-n}[D_{i}^{1}])}{\mathrm{Vol}(f^{-n}[D_{i}^{2}])}=e^{\pm 2\epsilon^{2}(\frac{d(x,y)}{(p^{s}\wedge p^{u})^{3}})^{\frac{\beta}{4}}}.

Shown similarly as in Corollary
7.3
.

Now that we have useful covers in our arsenal, and good estimates on the leaf measures of the cover elements, we wish to begin the proof of the absolute continuity property. If the leaf volume of
A(u)
is
0
in both leaves, we are done. So we may assume that the volume in one of them is positive- w.l.o.g
V^{u}((u_{i}^{1})_{i\leq 0})
- and get estimates for the measure in the other. We start by recalling a useful measure theoretical lemma:

Lemma 7.6
.

Given
\delta>0
, let
U_{\delta}^{1}
be a
\delta
-neighborhood of
V^{u}((u_{i}^{1})_{i\leq 0})\cap A(u)
in the leaf
V^{u}((u_{i}^{1})_{i\leq 0})
. Then
\exists\delta_{0}>0
s.t
\forall\delta\in(0,\delta_{0}]

\frac{\mathrm{Vol}(A(u))}{\mathrm{Vol}(U_{\delta}^{1})}\geq e^{-2\epsilon^{2}(\frac{2d_{C_{0}}(V^{u}((u_{i}^{1})_{i\leq 0}),V^{u}((u_{i}^{2})_{i\leq 0}))}{(p^{s}\wedge p^{u})^{3}})^{\frac{\beta}{4}}},

where
\mathrm{Vol}
is the intrinsic
V^{u}((u_{i}^{1})_{i\leq 0})
-volume.

In particular,
\delta_{0}
does not depend on
n
. Therefore,
\forall\delta\in(0,\delta_{0})
we may choose
n_{\delta}
s.t
\forall n\geq n_{\delta}
,

(61)

\bigcup_{i=1}^{\ell}f^{-n}[D_{i}^{2}]\subseteq U_{\delta}^{2},

where
U^{2}_{\delta}
is the
\delta
-neighborhood of
V^{u}((u_{i}^{2})_{i\leq 0})\cap A(u)
in the leaf
V^{u}((u_{i}^{2})_{i\leq 0})
.

Corollary 7.7
.

\forall\delta\in(0,\delta_{0})

\frac{\mathrm{Vol}(U^{2}_{\delta})}{\mathrm{Vol}(U^{1}_{\delta})}\geq e^{-4\epsilon^{2}(\frac{2d_{C_{0}}(V^{u}((u_{i}^{1})_{i\leq 0}),V^{u}((u_{i}^{2})_{i\leq 0}))}{(p^{s}\wedge p^{u})^{3}})^{\frac{\beta}{4}}}.

Proof.

Let
n_{\delta}
as in (
61
). Then

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{Vol}(U^{2}_{\delta})}{\mathrm{Vol}(U^{1}_{\delta})}=\frac{\mathrm{Vol}(U^{2}_{\delta})}{\mathrm{Vol}(\bigcup_{i=1}^{\ell}f^{-n}[D_{i}^{2}])}\cdot\frac{\mathrm{Vol}(\bigcup_{i=1}^{\ell}f^{-n}[D_{i}^{2}])}{\mathrm{Vol}(\bigcup_{i=1}^{\ell}f^{-n}[D_{i}^{1}])}\cdot\frac{\mathrm{Vol}(\bigcup_{i=1}^{\ell}f^{-n}[D_{i}^{1}])}{\mathrm{Vol}(U^{1}_{\delta})}.

By (
61
), the first factor is greater or equal to
1
. By Lemma
7.6
, the third factor is greater or equal to
e^{-2\epsilon^{2}(\frac{2d_{C_{0}}(V^{u}((u_{i}^{1})_{i\leq 0}),V^{u}((u_{i}^{2})_{i\leq 0}))}{(p^{s}\wedge p^{u})^{3}})^{\frac{\beta}{4}}}
. By Lemma
7.4
and Corollary
7.4
,

\frac{\mathrm{Vol}(\bigcup_{i=1}^{\ell}f^{-n}[D_{i}^{2}])}{\mathrm{Vol}(\bigcup_{i=1}^{\ell}f^{-n}[D_{i}^{1}])}=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\mathrm{Vol}(f^{-n}[D_{i}^{2}])}{\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\mathrm{Vol}(f^{-n}[D_{i}^{1}])}=e^{\pm 2\epsilon^{2}(\frac{2d_{C_{0}}(V^{u}((u_{i}^{1})_{i\leq 0}),V^{u}((u_{i}^{2})_{i\leq 0}))}{(p^{s}\wedge p^{u})^{3}})^{\frac{\beta}{4}}}.

This completes the proof.
∎

Corollary 7.8
.

\mathrm{Vol}(A(u)\cap V^{u}((u_{i}^{2})_{i\leq 0}))>0
.

Proof.

\Sigma
is locally compact,
\pi
is uniformly continuous, and so
A(u)
is compact. Therefore, if
\mathrm{Vol}(A(u)\cap V^{u}((u_{i}^{2})_{i\leq 0}))=0
, then
\lim_{\delta\to 0}\mathrm{Vol}(U^{2}_{\delta})=0
as well, but this is a contradiction to Corollary
7.7
.
∎

Now we may apply Lemma
7.6
to the set
A(u)\cap V^{u}((u_{i}^{2})_{\leq 0})
, and get a
\delta_{1}\in(0,\delta_{0}]
s.t
\forall\delta\in(0,\delta_{1})
,

\frac{\mathrm{Vol}(A(u))}{\mathrm{Vol}(U_{\delta}^{2})}\geq e^{-2\epsilon^{2}(\frac{2d_{C_{0}}(V^{u}((u_{i}^{1})_{i\leq 0}),V^{u}((u_{i}^{2})_{i\leq 0}))}{(p^{s}\wedge p^{u})^{3}})^{\frac{\beta}{4}}},

where
\mathrm{Vol}
is the intrinsic
V^{u}((u_{i}^{2})_{i\leq 0})
-volume.

Therefore, we may use Lemma
7.7
with replacing the roles of
U^{1}_{\delta}
and
U^{2}_{\delta}
, for all
\delta\in(0,\delta_{1})
,

(62)

\frac{\mathrm{Vol}(U^{1}_{\delta})}{\mathrm{Vol}(U^{2}_{\delta})}=e^{\pm 4\epsilon^{2}(\frac{2d_{C_{0}}(V^{u}((u_{i}^{1})_{i\leq 0}),V^{u}((u_{i}^{2})_{i\leq 0}))}{(p^{s}\wedge p^{u})^{3}})^{\frac{\beta}{4}}}.

It then follows from Lemma
7.6
and Corollary
7.7
that
\forall\delta\in(0,\delta_{1})
,

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{Vol}(V^{u}((u_{i}^{1})_{i\leq 0})\cap A(u))}{\mathrm{Vol}(V^{u}((u_{i}^{2})_{i\leq 0})\cap A(u))}=

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{Vol}(V^{u}((u_{i}^{1})_{i\leq 0})\cap A(u))}{\mathrm{Vol}(U^{1}_{\delta})}\cdot\frac{\mathrm{Vol}(U^{1}_{\delta})}{\mathrm{Vol}(U^{2}_{\delta})}\cdot\frac{\mathrm{Vol}(U^{2}_{\delta})}{\mathrm{Vol}(V^{u}((u_{i}^{2})_{i\leq 0})\cap A(u))}

(63)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{\pm 6\epsilon^{2}(\frac{2d_{C_{0}}(V^{u}((u_{i}^{1})_{i\leq 0}),V^{u}((u_{i}^{2})_{i\leq 0}))}{(p^{s}\wedge p^{u})^{3}})^{\frac{\beta}{4}}}.

7.3.
Construction of strictly weak leaves

In §
3
we construct weak manifolds- local stable and unstable leaves for orbits shadowed by
I
-chains. In particular, this construction does not make use of an asymptotic exponential contraction on the stable or unstable subspaces of the shadowed orbit; when exponentially-fast contracting manifolds are the standard stable and unstable manifolds in Pesin’s stable manifold theorem. A natural question which rises is whether there are weak manifolds, which cannot be constructed using Pesin’s stable manifolds theorem. That is, local stable and unstable leaves which exhibit strictly sub-exponential contraction. Such manifolds are
strictly weak
stable and unstable manifolds.

In this chapter we give a general result regarding the existence of strictly weak leaves. It uses the observation that every hyperbolic periodic orbit, and every uniformly hyperbolic measure, can be coded by our coding. In §
8
we discuss a general condition for the “codability” of measures- i.e property of having a lift in our coding space.

Given
x\in\mathrm{RST}
, let
W^{s}(x):=\bigcup_{n\geq 0}f^{-n}[V^{s}(f^{n}(x))]
,
W^{u}(x):=\bigcup_{n\geq 0}f^{n}[V^{s}(f^{-n}(x))]
, which are increasing unions.

Definition 7.9

(Ergodic homoclinic class)
.

Let
p
be a periodic hyperbolic point. Define

H(p):=\{x\in\mathrm{RST}:W^{u}(x)\pitchfork W^{s}(p)\neq\varnothing,W^{u}(p)\pitchfork W^{s}(x)\neq\varnothing\}.

This definition is due to Rodriguez-Hertz, Rodriguez-Hertz, Tahzibi, and Ures (
[
RHRHTU11
]
).

Definition 7.10

(Maximal irreducible components)
.

Let
\mathcal{R}
be partition constructed in §
6.2
.

(1)

We say that
R\overset{n}{\to}S
if there exists a word of length
n
,
(w_{0},w_{1},\ldots,w_{n-2},w_{n-1})
, s.t
w_{i}\in\mathcal{R}
for all
i\leq n-1
,
w_{0}=R
and
w_{n_{1}}=S
, and for all
i\leq n-2

f^{-1}[w_{i+1}]\cap w_{i}\neq\varnothing
.

(2)

We write for two partition elements
R,S\in\mathcal{R}
, we write
R\sim S
if
\exists n,m\geq 0
s.t
R\overset{n}{\to}S
and
S\overset{m}{\to}R
.
\sim
defines a symmetric and transitive relation on
\mathcal{R}
. When restricting to
R\in\mathcal{R}
s.t
R\sim R
,
\sim
defines an equivalence relation.

(3)

Let
R\in\mathcal{R}
s.t
R\sim R
, then
\langle R\rangle
is the equivalence class of
R
w.r.t
\sim
.

We call equivalence classes of the equivalence relation
\sim
, maximal irreducible components.

Lemma 7.11

(Transitive coding)
.

Given an ergodic homoclinc class, there is a maximal irreducible component of a
\widehat{\Sigma}
,
\widetilde{\Sigma}
, s.t
\widetilde{\Sigma}
can code every conservative measure on
H(p)
. In particular, it codes every periodic orbit of every hyperbolic periodic point
q
which is homoclinically related to
p
.

This lemma is due to Buzzi, Crovisier, and Sarig
[
BCS
]
. This formulation of the lemma appears in
[
BO21
, § 5]
(with another set taking the role of
\mathrm{RST}
in the definition of ergodic homoclinic classes, but both sets carry all uniformly hyperbolic measures and it is sufficient for the proof).

Definition 7.12
.

Given an ergodic invariant hyperbolic measure
\mu
, we write
\chi^{u}(\mu):=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\max(\chi_{i}(\mu),0)
and
\chi^{s}(\mu):=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\max(-\chi_{i}(\mu),0)
, where
\chi_{1}(\mu),\ldots,\chi_{d}(\mu)
are the Lyapunov exponents of
\mu
(with multiplicity). If
p
is a hyperbolic periodic point,
\chi^{s/u}(p)
means
\chi^{s/u}(\mu_{p})
, where
\mu_{p}
is the invariant measure supported on the orbit of
p
.
13
13

13

The Lyapunov exponents of a periodic orbit are well-defined, as working in coordinates, the differential can be put in a Jordan-block form, with respective eigenvalues. See
[
BO21
, Lemma 5.4]

for more details.

Proposition 7.13

(Existence of strictly weak foliations)
.

Let
H(p)
s.t
\inf\{\chi^{s}(q):q\in H(p)\text{ periodic}\}=0
. Then
H(p)
admits strictly weak stable manifolds. A similar statement applies to strictly weak unstable manifolds.

Proof.

By Lemma
7.11
we have a maximal irreducible component
\widetilde{\Sigma}
which codes all
q\in H(p)
which are periodic. Therefore, there are periodic words in
\widetilde{\Sigma}
,
\{\underline{w}^{(i)}\}_{i\geq 0}
,
\underline{w}^{(i)}=(w^{(i)}_{0},\ldots,w^{(i)}_{l_{i}-1})
s.t the Lyapunov exponents
\chi_{i}:=\chi^{s}(\underline{w}^{(i)})\to 0
.

Fix
w_{0}^{(0)}
and denote it by
R
. Let
n_{i}:=\min\{n\geq 0:R\overset{n}{\to}w_{0}^{(i)}\}
(which is well-defined since
\widetilde{\Sigma}
is an irreducible component); and let
\underline{c}^{(i)}
be a connecting word of such length. Similarly, let
m_{i}:=\min\{m\geq 0:w_{0}^{(i)}\overset{m}{\to}R\}
; and let
\underline{d}^{(i)}
be a connecting word of such length.

Define for all
i\geq 1
,

(64)

p_{i}:=\min\{p\geq 0:p\cdot l_{i}\geq\frac{1}{\chi_{i}}\cdot d\cdot\log M_{f}\cdot(m_{i}+p_{i-1}+n_{i+1}+l_{i+1})\}.

Consider the chain

\underline{R}:=(\underline{w}^{(0)})\cdot\underline{c}^{(1)}\cdot(\underline{w}^{(1)})^{p_{1}}\cdot\underline{d}^{(1)}\cdots(\underline{w}^{(n)})\cdot\underline{c}^{(n+1)}\cdot(\underline{w}^{(n+1)})^{p_{n+1}}\cdot\underline{d}^{(n+1)}\cdots,

where
\cdot
means a concatenation, and
(\cdot)^{\ell}
means a concatenation of a word to itself
\ell
times. By construction, this is as an admissible chain, which returns to
R
infinitely often. Take any recurrent extension of
\underline{R}
to the negative coordinates,
\underline{R}^{\pm}\in\widehat{\Sigma}^{\#}
, and write
x:=\widehat{\pi}(\underline{R}^{\pm})
.

For simplicity of presentation, we first prove the proposition under the assumption that
\mathrm{dim}H^{s}(x)=1
, and then show how it extends to higher dimensions. When
\mathrm{dim}H^{s}=1
, there is only one normalized stable tangent vector at the point (up to a sign), denoted by
\omega^{s}(\cdot)
. Then the quantity
S^{2}(\cdot)
is well-defined. Consider the chain
\underline{w}^{(i,\pm)}
which is the periodic extension of
\underline{w}^{(i)}
to
\widehat{\Sigma}
. Write
w_{j}^{(i)}=\psi_{x_{j}^{(i)}}^{p^{s,(i)}_{j},p^{u,(i)}_{j}}
. Then (
3
) tells us that

\displaystyle\log|d_{\widehat{\pi}(\underline{w}^{(i,\pm)})}f^{l_{i}}\omega^{s}(\widehat{\pi}(\underline{w}^{(i,\pm)}))|=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{l_{i}-1}\log\sqrt{1-\frac{2}{S^{2}(f^{j}(\widehat{\pi}(\underline{w}^{(i,\pm)})))}}+\log S(\widehat{\pi}(\underline{w}^{(i,\pm)}))-\log S(\widehat{\pi}(\underline{w}^{(i,\pm)})),

(65)

\displaystyle\log|d_{f^{t_{i}}(x)}f^{l_{i}}\omega^{s}(f^{t_{i}(x)})|=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{l_{i}-1}\log\sqrt{1-\frac{2}{S^{2}(f^{j+t_{i}}(x))}}+\log S(f^{t_{i}}(x))-\log S(f^{t_{i}+l_{i}}(x)).

The proof of Lemma
4.7
allows to compare multiplicatively the quantities of both equations of (
7.3
), through the centers of charts
\{x^{(i)}_{j}\}_{j=t_{i}}^{t_{i}+l_{i}}
(notice
S^{2}(\cdot)\geq\frac{2}{1-M_{f}^{-2}}
). The choice of
p_{i}
in (
64
) guarantees, together with the fact that
\chi_{i}\to 0
, that
\chi^{s}(x)=0
.

Now, in order to see the proof when
\mathrm{dim}H^{s}(x)>1
, the role of
\omega^{s}(\cdot)
is replaced by the unique (up to sign) normalized volume form on
H^{s}(\cdot)
. One can define

\widehat{S}^{2}(x):=2\sum_{m\geq 0}|d_{x}f^{m}\omega^{s}(x)|^{2},

which is finite by the Hadamard inequality and the fact that
x\in 0
-summ. (
3
) derives similarly
|d_{x}f\omega^{s}(x)|=\frac{\widehat{S}(x)}{\widehat{S}(f(x))}\sqrt{1-\frac{2}{\widehat{S}^{2}(x)}}
, and so (
7.3
) follows. The comparison of the two quantities in (
7.3
) through the center of charts is done similarly by an analogous version of Lemma
4.7
, which follows from Lemma
4.7
: the map
\pi_{x}^{i,j}:H^{s}(f^{t_{i}+j}(x))\to H^{s}(x_{j}^{(i)})
satisfies
\frac{|\pi_{x}^{i,j}\xi|}{|\xi|}=e^{\pm Q_{\epsilon}(x_{j})^{\frac{\beta}{2}-\frac{1}{2\gamma}}}
(Lemma
4.6
). Therefore
\mathrm{Jac}(\pi_{x}^{i,j})=e^{\pm dQ_{\epsilon}(x_{j})^{\frac{\beta}{2}-\frac{1}{2\gamma}}}
; whence the vector estimates of Lemma
4.7
pull to volume form estimates as well. Strictly sub-exponential contraction of the volume form on
V^{s}(\underline{R})
implies no tangent vector at
x
which contracts exponentially fast.
∎

8.
Codability

In §
6.2
we saw that
\widehat{\pi}[\widehat{\Sigma}^{\#}]=\mathrm{RST}
, so in particular we can code any measure which is carried by
\mathrm{RST}
. It is clear that every hyperbolic measure is carried by
0
-summ, but it is not clear for which ergodic measures almost every orbit is strongly temperable (recall Definition
2.8
).

First, we should notice that every periodic hyperbolic orbit, and every uniformly hyperbolic measure, are carried by
\mathrm{RST}
, since
\|C_{0}^{-1}(\cdot)\|
is bounded on every orbit. Being able to code uniformly hyperbolic measures is important, since it allows to “lift” the pressure of Hölder continuous potentials (as well as the geometric potential) over hyperbolic measures. That is,

\sup\{h_{\mu}(f)+\int\phi d\mu:\mu\text{ hyperbolic}\}=\sup\{h_{\mu}(f)+\int\phi d\mu:\mu\text{ uniformly hyperbolic}\},

for a Hölder continuous
\phi:M\to\mathbb{R}
. This is true since every hyperbolic measure can be coded by a TMS which lifts the potential
[
BO18
]
, and
[
Sar09
, Theorem 4.3]
shows that it can be approximated by the pressure over compact subshifts (which project to uniformly hyperbolic horseshoes).

In particular, since our coding lifts all hyperbolic periodic orbits, one can always construct invariant probability measures on the shift space. Such measures can even be Markov measures and fully supported on the shift space. They project to invariant probability measures on the manifold; and as long as the dynamics we code are not uniformly hyperbolic, these measures will be non-uniformly hyperbolic, and carried by
\mathrm{RST}
.

One can nonetheless ask,
in what generality can measures lift to
\widehat{\Sigma}
?
Below we give a general condition which is sufficient (though may be hard to test), and in a closing discussion later in this chapter, we review in what sense this condition is optimal.

Proposition 8.1
.

Let
x\in 0\mathrm{-summ}
. If
\|C_{0}^{-1}(f^{n}(x))\|^{2}=o(|n|)
,
n\to\pm\infty
, then
x\in\mathrm{ST}
.

In particular, this condition does not depend on the calibrating parameters
\gamma,\Gamma
, nor
\beta
, nor
\epsilon
. So an invariant probability measure carried by such orbits will be carried by any Markov partition constructed per this paper.

Proof.

Let
\delta>0
to be determined later. Let
n_{\delta}\in\mathbb{N}
s.t
\forall n\geq n_{\delta}
,
\|C_{0}^{-1}(f^{n}(x))\|^{2}\leq\delta n
. Set
c_{\delta}:=\min\{\frac{C_{\beta,\epsilon}}{\|C_{0}^{-1}(f^{n}(x))\|^{2\gamma}}:0\leq n\leq n_{\delta}\}
.

For every
n\geq 0
, define
q^{+}(f^{n}(x)):=\min\{c_{\delta},\frac{C_{\beta,\epsilon}}{(\delta n)^{\gamma}}\}\leq\frac{C_{\beta,\epsilon}}{\|C_{0}^{-1}(f^{n}(x))\|^{2\gamma}}
. In order to bound the variation of
q^{+}
, it is enough to bound the variation of
\frac{C_{\beta,\epsilon}}{(\delta n)^{\gamma}}
for
n\geq n_{\delta}+1
. Then for any
n\geq n_{\delta}+1
, (w.l.o.g
n_{\delta}
is big enough so
(1-\frac{1}{n})\geq e^{-2\frac{1}{n}}
)

\displaystyle\frac{\frac{C_{\beta,\epsilon}}{(\delta n)^{\gamma}}}{\frac{C_{\beta,\epsilon}}{(\delta(n-1))^{\gamma}}}\geq(\frac{n-1}{n})^{\gamma}\geq e^{-2\frac{\gamma}{n}}=e^{-2\gamma\cdot\frac{\delta}{(C_{\beta,\epsilon})^{\frac{1}{\gamma}}}\cdot(\frac{C_{\beta,\epsilon}}{(\delta n)^{\gamma}})^{\frac{1}{\gamma}}}\geq e^{-\Gamma\cdot(\frac{C_{\beta,\epsilon}}{(\delta n)^{\gamma}})^{\frac{1}{\gamma}}},

where the last inequality is when we choose
\delta>0
sufficiently small. Then we get,

I(\frac{C_{\beta,\epsilon}}{(\delta n)^{\gamma}})=\frac{C_{\beta,\epsilon}}{(\delta n)^{\gamma}}e^{\Gamma\cdot(\frac{C_{\beta,\epsilon}}{(\delta n)^{\gamma}})^{\frac{1}{\gamma}}}\geq\frac{C_{\beta,\epsilon}}{(\delta(n-1))^{\gamma}},\text{ whence }\frac{C_{\beta,\epsilon}}{(\delta n)^{\gamma}}\geq I^{-1}(\frac{C_{\beta,\epsilon}}{(\delta(n-1))^{\gamma}}).

This construction can be done for
f^{k}(x)
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, the same way. Therefore the Ledrappier formula is well-defined for every
k\in\mathbb{Z}
,

p^{s}_{k}:=\max\{t\in\mathcal{I}:I^{-n}(t)\leq\frac{C_{\beta,\epsilon}}{\|C_{0}^{-1}(f^{n}(f^{k}(x)))\|^{2\gamma}}\text{ for all }n\geq 0\}.

Similarly, the following is well-defined as well:

p^{u}_{k}:=\max\{t\in\mathcal{I}:I^{-n}(t)\leq\frac{C_{\beta,\epsilon}}{\|C_{0}^{-1}(f^{-n}(f^{k}(x)))\|^{2\gamma}}\text{ for all }n\geq 0\}.

It follows from the Ledrappier formula that for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}
,

(1)

p^{s}_{k},p^{u}_{k}\leq\frac{C_{\beta,\epsilon}}{\|C_{0}^{-1}(f^{k}(x))\|^{2\gamma}}
,

(2)

p^{u}_{k+1}=\min\{I(p_{k}^{u}),\frac{C_{\beta,\epsilon}}{\|C_{0}^{-1}(f^{k+1}(x))\|^{2\gamma}}\}
,
p^{s}_{k-1}=\min\{I(p_{k}^{s}),\frac{C_{\beta,\epsilon}}{\|C_{0}^{-1}(f^{k-1}(x))\|^{2\gamma}}\}
.

Therefore
q(f^{k}(x)):=\min\{p^{s}_{k},p^{u}_{k}\}
is a strongly tempered kernel for
x
, as in Lemma
2.24
.
∎

8.1.
Examples and discussion

In this section we consider a family of systems which is called
almost Anosov
, introduced by Hu and Young in
[
HY95
]
, and we mention a few results regarding our coding of these systems. The full details appear in an upcoming work. We then have a short discussion regarding optimality of our construction (i.e the notions of
0
-summability and strong temperability, and the condition in Proposition
8.1
for codability).

Definition 8.2

(Almost-Anosov)
.

Let
f\in\mathrm{Diff}^{r}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
,
r\geq 2
, be a topologically transitive diffeomorphism of the two dimensional torus. Assume that
f
admits an invariant splitting of the tangent bundle everywhere
T_{x}M=E^{s}(x)\oplus E^{u}(x)
and an indifferent fixed point
p
, s.t

(1)

\exists K^{s}<1
,
\forall x

|d_{x}f|_{E^{s}}|\leq K^{s}

(2)

\exists K^{u}(\cdot)\geq 1
continuous on
M
, s.t
\forall x

|d_{x}f|_{E^{u}}|\geq K^{u}(x)
,

(3)

For any
x\neq p
,
K^{u}(x)>1
, and
|d_{p}f|_{E^{u}}|=K^{u}(p)=1
.

Hu and Young introduce this class of systems to show that while these systems are very nicely behaving (“almost” uniform hyperbolicity), the admit no SRB measures.

The following result appears with details in an upcoming work, and for this paper we assume it.

Theorem 8.3
.

Given an almost Anosov diffeomorphism, every
x\neq p
is
0
-summable.

Corollary 8.4
.

Given an almost Anosov system, every invariant measure (
\neq\delta_{p}
) is carried by
\mathrm{RST}
(for any choice of the calibrating parameters), and hence codable.

Proof.

Let
\mu
be an invariant probability measure. W.l.o.g
\mu
is ergodic. If
\mu(p)=0
, then
\mu
-a.e point is summable by Theorem
8.3
. In addition,
|d_{x}f|_{H^{u}(x)}|\geq 1
everywhere, therefore

\displaystyle U^{2}(f(x))=

\displaystyle 2\sum_{m\geq 0}|d_{f(x)}f^{-m}|_{H^{u}(f(x))}|^{2}=2+|d_{f(x)}f^{-1}|_{H^{u}(f(x))}|^{2}\cdot 2\sum_{m\geq 0}|d_{x}f^{-m}|_{H^{u}(x)}|^{2}

(66)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2+|d_{f(x)}f^{-1}|_{H^{u}(f(x))}|^{2}\cdot U^{2}(x)\leq 2+U^{2}(x).

Thus,
U^{2}(f^{n}(x))\leq U^{2}(x)+2n
for all
n\geq 0
. Then
(U^{2}\circ f-U^{2})^{+}\in L^{1}(\mu)
. One can then show that necessarily
U^{2}\circ f-U^{2}\in L^{1}(\mu)
and
\int(U^{2}\circ f-U^{2})d\mu=0
(full details appear in the upcoming work where we show Theroem
8.3
). Then by the point-wise ergodic theorem,
\mu
-a.e,

\frac{U^{2}\circ f^{n}}{n}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}(U^{2}\circ f-U^{2})\circ f^{k}+\frac{U^{2}}{n}\to 0.

Similarly
U^{2}\circ f^{-n}=o(n)
,
n\to\infty
,
\mu
-a.e.

Finally, notice that the continuous splitting on a compact manifold implies that
\sphericalangle(H^{s}(x),H^{u}(x))
is bounded from below everywhere. In addition,
S^{2}\leq 2\sum_{m\geq 0}(K^{s})^{2m}=\frac{2}{1-(K^{s})^{2}}<\infty
. Then
\|C_{0}^{-1}(x)\|^{2}\leq C_{M}\cdot U^{2}(x)
for all
x\neq p
, where
C_{M}
is some global constant. Thus
\|C_{0}^{-1}\circ f^{n}\|^{2}=o(|n|)
,
n\to\pm\infty
,
\mu
-a.e. By Proposition
8.1
,
\mu
is carried by
\mathrm{RST}
and is codable.
∎

Remark:
We saw that
\widehat{\pi}[\widehat{\Sigma}^{\#}]=\mathrm{RST}
carries every invariant probability measure in the almost Anosov setup. Furthermore, we can see that in some cases we must have measures with arbitrarily small Lyapunov exponents, while we can code all of them simultaneously in a finite-to-one almost everywhere fashion. This follows from the fact that almost Anosov systems can be achieved as a perturbation (or a “slow-down”) of a mixing hyperbolic toral automorphism (which admit a mixing finite Markov partition). One can make the element of the partition which contains
p
arbitrarily small, call it
a
. Fix another element
b
, and construct a word from
b
to
b
with an arbitrarily long segment
a\ldots a
(admissible since
p
is a fixed point). Then This codes a periodic (hence hyperbolic) orbit, with arbitrarily small Lyapunov exponent. One then gets the existence of strictly weak manifolds by Proposition
7.13
.

After establishing the relationship between being codable and a sub-linear growth-rate of
\|C_{0}^{-1}(f^{n}(\cdot))\|^{2}
, one can ask the following three questions:

(1)

Can the definition of
0
-summable orbits be changed to get stronger contractions in charts? (Thus allowing us to require weaker properties than strong temperability).

(2)

Can the definition of strong temperability be weakened and still allow for a shadowing theory?

(3)

Is there a weaker condition than sub-linear growth-rate of
\|C_{0}^{-1}(f^{n}(\cdot))\|^{2}
to guarantee codability?

We will give non-formal heuristic answers to these questions, which justify the “optimality” of the notions in this paper.

(1)

The answer to this question has three parts. First, one can ask wether we can consider orbits which converge in a power higher than
2
. First notice that for every hyperbolic orbit, the sum converges regardless of the power, and that every periodic orbit which converges in some power must be hyperbolic. Furthermore, every measure which is uniformly summable in some power must be hyperbolic. Therefore one does not lose much by choosing the power
2
, but gains a lot by the fact that the parallelogram law dictates that only for the power
2
, the scaling functions will constitute a norm on the tangent space which is induced by an inner-product. This bilinear form which induces the scaling functions (i.e the Lyapunov inner-product) is paramount to our construction of the Lyapunov change of coordinates.

One can also ask if perhaps adding an exponential term in the definition of summability, which depends on the Lyapunov exponent of the orbit, can yield a simpler construction while still coding all “sufficiently nice” hyperbolic orbits. The heuristic answer is that this is conceptually wrong. A Lyapunov exponent is not a property of a point (resp. symbol), it is a property of the orbit (resp. chain). Imposing an edge condition which requires some correspondence between the Lyapunov exponents of neighboring charts, goes against the Markov structure which we sought to construct. Without imposing some correspondence on the exponents through an edge condition, one cannot use a composition of Pesin charts
d_{x}f^{n}=C_{0}(f^{n}(x))\circ(C_{0}^{-1}(f^{n}(x))d_{f^{n-1}(x)}fC_{0}(f^{n-1}x))\circ\cdots\circ(C_{0}^{-1}(f(x))d_{x}fC_{0}(x))\circ C_{0}^{-1}(x)
without accumulating errors.

Lastly, once can ask if some weights can be added in the definition of summability to aid/hinder convergence. A natural choice of such weights which has been studied before is exponential weights. Their short-comes are clear, as they restrict us to
\chi
-hyperbolic orbits, where
\chi
depends on the exponent in the weights. If one chooses rates where the ratio between them does not go to a constant number, then shifting the sequence does not change it by a factor, and again, creates discrepancy. For these heuristic reason, the notion of
0
-summability is quite natural.

(2)

(
8.1
) shows us that even for a family of systems which are very nicely behaving, one gets naturally the relation
\frac{1}{U^{2\gamma}\circ f}\geq\frac{1}{U^{2\gamma}}e^{-2(\frac{1}{U^{2\gamma}})^{\frac{1}{\gamma}}}
. Therefore, heuristically, when we wish to give a general description to a phenomenon which has a relatively easily understood example, we cannot expect behavior nicer than that of the example generally. It turns out that strong temperability, which utilizes exactly the relation of the form
\frac{C_{\beta,\epsilon}}{\|C_{0}^{-1}\|^{2\gamma}\circ f}\geq\frac{C_{\beta,\epsilon}}{\|C_{0}^{-1}\|^{2\gamma}}e^{-\Gamma(\frac{C_{\beta,\epsilon}}{\|C_{0}^{-1}\|^{2\gamma}\circ f})^{\frac{1}{\gamma}}}
, is sufficient for a shadowing theory in the general case.

(3)

In Lemma
2.6
we saw that being strongly temperable implies that
\|C_{0}^{-1})\|^{2\gamma}\circ f^{n}=O(n^{\gamma})
, where the big
O
constant depends on
\gamma
and
\Gamma
. Therefore an orbit which is coded by our coding or is strongly temperable for any choice of the calibrating parameters, will have to satisfy
\|C_{0}^{-1})\|^{2}\circ f^{n}=o(n)
. This implies that this condition captures a significant portion of the orbits we wish to code.

9.
Temperability and rate of contraction

In this section we present a relationship between two properties which are studied in this paper: Temperability and the rate of contraction on stable and unstable manifolds.

We begin by showing that as long as an orbit visits a level set of
S^{2}
with some positive frequency, it must contract tangent vectors on the stable direction exponentially fast asymptotically (similarly for
U^{2}
and the unstable direction). This shows us that hyperbolicity emerges as a phenomenon of positive recurrence to level sets of the scaling function. In particular, every invariant probability measure carried by
0
-summ must be hyperbolic. However, the lack of positive recurrence does not imply the lack of recurrence.

We therefore continue to study the rate of contraction of tangent vectors in the stable or unstable subspaces, without assuming positive recurrence. We show that the temperability properties induce a rate of contraction. By temperability, we mean asymptotic growth-rate bounds for
\|C_{0}^{-1}\|^{2}
(or
S^{2}/U^{2}
). We saw in Proposition
8.1
that a sub-linear growth-rate implies being strongly temperable. Proposition
9.2
considers more rates.

9.1.
Hyperbolicity as positive recurrence of the scaling functions

Proposition 9.1
.

Let
x\in 0\mathrm{-summ}
, and let
\xi\in H^{s}(x)
s.t
|\xi|=1
. Write
\xi_{k}:=\frac{d_{x}f^{k}\xi}{|d_{x}f^{k}\xi|}
. Then if
\lim\frac{1}{n}\log S(f^{n}(x))=0
,

(1)

\limsup\limits_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log|d_{x}f^{n}\xi|<0
if and only if
\exists C>0
s.t
\liminf\limits_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\#\{0\leq i\leq n:S^{2}(f^{k}(x),\xi_{k})\leq C\}>0
.

(2)

\liminf\limits_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log|d_{x}f^{n}\xi|<0
if and only if
\exists C>0
s.t
\limsup\limits_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\#\{0\leq i\leq n:S^{2}(f^{k}(x),\xi_{k})\leq C\}>0
.

A similar statement holds for the backwards orbit and
U^{2}
.

Proof.

Write
\zeta^{u}_{n}:=\log\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-\frac{2}{S^{2}(f^{n}(x),\xi_{n})}}}\in(0,\log M_{f}]
. By (
3
),
\forall n\geq 0
,

(67)

\frac{1}{n}\log|d_{x}f^{n}\xi|=-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\zeta^{u}_{k}+\frac{1}{n}\log S(x,\xi)-\frac{1}{n}\log S(f^{n}(x),\xi_{n})\leq-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\zeta^{u}_{k}+\frac{1}{n}\log S(x,\xi).

Then the following claim implies the proposition.

Claim:

(1)

\limsup\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\zeta^{u}_{i}>0
if and only if
\exists\delta>0
s.t
\limsup\frac{1}{n}\#\{0\leq i\leq n:S^{2}(f^{i}(x),\xi_{i})\leq\frac{1}{\delta}\}>\delta
,

(2)

\liminf\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\zeta^{u}_{i}>0
if and only if
\exists\delta>0
s.t
\liminf\frac{1}{n}\#\{0\leq i\leq n:S^{2}(f^{i}(x),\xi_{i})\leq\frac{1}{\delta}\}>\delta
.

Proof: We start by showing item (1). Let
n_{k}
be a subsequence.

\Rightarrow
:
Assume for contradiction that for any
\delta>0
there exists
k_{\delta}\geq 0
s.t
\forall k\geq k_{\delta}
,

\frac{1}{n_{k}}\#\{i\leq n_{k}:S^{2}(f^{i}(x),\xi_{i})\leq\frac{1}{\delta}\}\leq\delta.

Notice, if
S^{2}(f^{i}(x),\xi_{i})\geq\frac{1}{\delta}
, then
\zeta^{u}_{i}\leq\log\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-2\delta}}\leq 2\delta
(for all sufficiently small
\delta>0
). Therefore,

(68)

\frac{1}{n_{k}}\sum_{i=0}^{n_{k}-1}\zeta^{u}_{i}=\frac{1}{n_{k}}\sum_{i\leq n_{k}:S^{2}(f^{i}(x),\xi_{i})<\frac{1}{\delta}}\zeta^{u}_{i}+\frac{1}{n_{k}}\sum_{i\leq n_{k}:S^{2}(f^{i}(x),\xi_{i})\geq\frac{1}{\delta}}\zeta^{u}_{i}\leq\log M_{f}\cdot\delta+2\delta\leq(\log M_{f}+2)\cdot\delta.

This is a contradiction when
0<\delta<\frac{1}{2}\frac{\limsup\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\zeta^{u}_{i}}{\log M_{f}+2}=\frac{1}{2}\frac{\lim\frac{1}{n_{k}}\sum_{i=0}^{n_{k}-1}\zeta^{u}_{i}}{\log M_{f}+2}
, for some subsequence
n_{k}
.

\Leftarrow
:
Assume that
\exists\delta>0
s.t
\limsup\frac{1}{n}\#\{0\leq i\leq n:S^{2}(f^{i}(x),\xi_{i})\leq\frac{1}{\delta}\}>\delta
. Notice that if
S^{2}(f^{i}(x),\xi)\leq\frac{1}{\delta}
then
\zeta^{u}_{i}\geq\log\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-2\delta}}\geq\delta
. Then by (
68
), for
n_{k}
s.t
\frac{1}{n_{k}}\#\{0\leq i\leq n_{k}:S^{2}(f^{i}(x),\xi_{i})\leq\frac{1}{\delta}\}>\frac{\delta}{2}
,

\frac{1}{n_{k}}\sum_{i=0}^{n_{k}-1}\zeta^{u}_{i}>\frac{1}{n_{k}}\sum_{i\leq n_{k}:S^{2}(f^{i}(x),\xi_{i})<\frac{1}{\delta}}\zeta^{u}_{i}\geq\frac{\delta}{2}\cdot\delta>0.

This concludes item (1). The proof of item (2) is similar, and we leave it to the reader. ∎

Remark:
(
67
) shows that for the “if” direction, the assumption
\lim\frac{1}{n}\log S\circ f^{n}=0
is not needed. This is in fact the direction which implies how hyperbolicity emerges from positive recurrence.

9.2.
Temperability and rate of contraction

Let
F(x):=e^{\alpha x}
, where
\alpha,x>0
. We get that
\frac{F(x)}{F^{\prime}(x)}=\frac{1}{\alpha}<\infty
. That is, for an exponential
F
, this quantity is bounded away from infinity uniformly in
x
(however big the bound may be).

We wish to consider all rates which are strictly weaker than exponential, in the following sense: Let
F:\mathbb{R}^{+}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
be a differentiable function s.t

\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{F(x)}{F^{\prime}(x)}=\infty,

and assume that
\frac{F(x)}{F^{\prime}(x)}
is non-decreasing. So, as long as
\frac{F(x)}{F^{\prime}(x)}
grows and is unbounded (no matter how slowly it grows), we consider it. The following proposition tells us that for any rate which is weaker than exponential in the sense above, there is a temperability rate which implies such contraction on the stable or unstable subspaces.

Proposition 9.2
.

Let
F:\mathbb{R}^{+}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
differentiable s.t
\frac{F(x)}{F^{\prime}(x)}\uparrow\infty
. Let
x\in 0\mathrm{-summ}
,
\xi\in H^{s}(x)
s.t
|\xi|=1
. Write
\xi_{n}:=\frac{d_{x}f^{n}\xi}{|d_{x}f^{n}\xi|}
, and assume that
\exists C>0
s.t
\forall n\geq 0
,
\frac{1}{2}S^{2}(f^{n}(x),\xi_{n})\leq C\frac{F(n)}{F^{\prime}(n)}
. Then
|d_{x}f^{n}\xi|=O(\frac{1}{(F(n))^{\frac{1-\tau}{C}}})
,
\forall\tau>0
. A similar statement holds for contraction on
H^{u}(x)
.

Proof.

We start by proving an inequality. Let
n_{1}>n_{0}\geq 1
. Write
G(t):=\frac{F^{\prime}(t)}{F(t)}
, which is monotone, then,

(69)

|\int_{n_{0}}^{n_{1}}G(t)dt-\sum_{k=n_{0}}^{n_{1}-1}G(k)|=\sum_{k=n_{0}}^{n_{1}-1}G(k)-\int_{k}^{k+1}G(t)dt\leq\sum_{k=n_{0}}^{n_{1}-1}G(k)-G(k+1)=G(n_{0})-G(n_{1})\leq G(1).

We now may continue with proving the proof. Write
\zeta_{n}^{u}:=\log\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-\frac{2}{S^{2}(f^{n}(x),\xi_{n})}}}
. Let
\tau>0
, then for all
n\geq n_{x}
, where
n_{x}
is large enough so
\frac{F^{\prime}(n_{x})}{CF(n_{x})}
is sufficiently small,
\zeta^{u}_{n}\geq\frac{(1-\tau)}{C}\frac{F^{\prime}(n)}{F(n)}
. Then, for all
n\geq n_{x}
, by (
67
),

\displaystyle|d_{x}f^{n}\xi|\leq

\displaystyle S(x,\xi)\cdot\exp\left(-\sum_{k=n_{x}}^{n-1}\frac{(1-\tau)}{C}\frac{F^{\prime}(k)}{F(k)}\right)\leq S(x)\cdot\exp\left(-\frac{(1-\tau)}{C}\sum_{k=n_{x}}^{n-1}\frac{F^{\prime}(k)}{F(k)}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle S(x)\cdot\exp\left(\frac{F^{\prime}(1)}{F(1)C}-\frac{(1-\tau)}{C}\int_{n_{x}}^{n}\frac{F^{\prime}(t)}{F(t)}dt\right)=C_{x}\cdot\exp\left(-\frac{(1-\tau)}{C}\int_{n_{x}}^{n}(\log F(t))^{\prime}dt\right)\text{ }\because\eqref{finallyIwannaGoHome}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle C_{x}\cdot\exp\left(-\frac{(1-\tau)}{C}(\log F(n)-\log F(n_{x}))dt\right)=C_{x}^{\prime}\cdot\frac{1}{(F(n))^{\frac{1-\tau}{C}}}.

In particular, when
\|C_{0}^{-1}(f^{n}(x))\|^{2}=o(n)
then
\|d_{x}f^{n}|_{H^{s}(x)}\|
decreases super-polynomially. Unfortunately, this relationship is not easily reversible. That is, super-polynomial contraction does not necessarily imply strong temperability, since we cannot commute a bound on the sum in the exponent, into a bound onto its terms (while the other way around works).

∎
